॥ तन्त्रसारसङ्ग्रहः ॥

Tantrasāra-Saṅgraha

of Śrī Ānandatīrtha Bhagavatpādācārya (Madhvācārya)

Every one of the attached reader’s 401 numbered verses: exact Devanagari source, IAST, and complete English meaning with no summarization.

The verse divisions and numbering follow the attached Sarvamūla reader exactly: 75 verses in Chapter 1, 24 in Chapter 2, 141 in Chapter 3, and 161 in Chapter 4. The complete Tantrasāra-vivṛti edition supplies the literal translations and optional word/compound explanations. Where that edition joins syntax across different verse boundaries, the affected source verses are kept together as one clearly marked translation unit so every Sanskrit word remains accounted for.

401 verses

Chapter 1प्रथमोऽध्यायः

मङ्गलाचरणम्

Chapter 1 · verse 1
1.1
जयत्यब्जभवेशेन्द्रवन्दितः कमलापतिः।
अनन्तविभवानन्दशक्तिज्ञानादिसद्गुणः॥ १॥
jayatyabjabhaveśendravanditaḥ kamalāpatiḥ|
anantavibhavānandaśaktijñānādisadguṇaḥ||
1||
Exact English meaningSupreme in victory stands the Lord of Kamalā (Lakṣmī), adored by the lotus-born Brahmā, by Īśa (Rudra) and by Indra — He whose flawless attributes, His majesty, bliss, power, knowledge and the rest, are infinite.

अष्टमहामन्त्राः

Chapter 1 · verse 2
1.2
विधिं विधाय सर्गादौ तेन पृष्टोऽब्जलोचनः।
आह देवो रमोत्सङ्गविलसत्पादपल्लवः॥ २॥
vidhiṃ vidhāya sargādau tena pṛṣṭo’bjalocanaḥ|
āha devo ramotsaṅgavilasatpādapallavaḥ||
2||
Exact English meaningAt the beginning of creation the lotus-eyed God, having fashioned Vidhi (Brahmā) and being questioned by him, spoke — He, the Deva whose bud-like feet lie gleaming upon the lap of Ramā.
Word and compound explanation
Now, in order to establish the authoritativeness of the revered Tantrasāra, he declares the excellence of its speaker and of its hearer: the verse "vidhiṃ vidhāya." At the beginning of creation — at the commencement of the world's emanation — having "made" Vidhi, that is Brahmā, having brought him forth from the lotus of His own navel, the Deva — Śrī Viṣṇu, possessed of manifold attributes such as divine play — "spoke": He instructed Brahmā. Of what sort was He? "Questioned by him," by that Brahmā; supply "the Tantrasāra" (as the content asked about), since the verb "to ask" governs two objects. Again, of what sort? "His bud-like feet gleaming upon the lap of Ramā" — He whose two sprout-soft feet lie shining on Ramā's lap. Thus, seated wholly at ease, He taught Vidhi, His own son; hence there is no room whatever for the suspicion of deception or the like (in this teaching). (2)
Chapter 1 · verse 3
1.3
अहमेकोऽखिलगुणो वाचकः प्रणवो मम।
अकाराद्यतिशान्तान्तः सोऽयमष्टाक्षरो मतः॥ ३॥
ahameko’khilaguṇo vācakaḥ praṇavo mama|
akārādyatiśāntāntaḥ so’yamaṣṭākṣaro mataḥ||
3||
Exact English meaningI alone am possessed of the totality of qualities; the praṇava (Oṁ) is My designator. Beginning with akāra and ending with atiśānta, it is held to consist of eight syllables.
Word and compound explanation
What did He say? The verse "aham." He whose qualities are akhila — full, infinite — is so described. Such a one am I, eka, alone; verily no other is such. As the Amarakośa says: "eka means chief, other, and sole." The praṇava is My vācaka, My designator — that which declares My possession of infinite qualities. That same praṇava "begins with akāra and ends with atiśānta": akāra stands at its beginning and atiśānta at its end. Through its division into akāra, ukāra, makāra, bindu, nāda, ghoṣa, śānta and atiśānta, it is "held" — known, celebrated — as having eight syllables. (3)
Chapter 1 · verse 4
1.4
स विश्वतैजसप्राज्ञतुरीयात्मान्तरात्मनाम्।
परमात्मज्ञानात्मयुजां मद्रूपाणां च वाचकः॥ ४॥
sa viśvataijasaprājñaturīyātmāntarātmanām|
paramātmajñānātmayujāṃ madrūpāṇāṃ ca vācakaḥ||
4||
Exact English meaningThat (praṇava) is the designator of My forms — Viśva, Taijasa, Prājña, Turīya, and Ātman, together with Antarātman, Paramātman and Jñānātman.
Word and compound explanation
He states the deities expressed by the syllables of the praṇava: the verse "sa." In order, through akāra and the rest, it is the designator of My own forms beginning with Viśva and ending with Jñānātman. The word "ca" ("and") means: not of Me alone, but also of these My forms. (4)
Chapter 1 · verse 5
1.5
तद्रूपभेदाः पञ्चाशन्मूर्तयो मम चापराः।
पञ्चाशद्वर्णवाच्यास्ता वर्णास्तारार्णभेदिताः॥ ५॥
tadrūpabhedāḥ pañcāśanmūrtayo mama cāparāḥ|
pañcāśadvarṇavācyāstā varṇāstārārṇabheditāḥ||
5||
Exact English meaningAnd My other forms, the fifty mūrtis, are differentiations of those forms; they are expressed by the fifty letters — and those letters are themselves differentiated out of the syllables of the tāra (praṇava).
Word and compound explanation
He now declares the manifestation, from the syllables of the praṇava and likewise from those (eight forms), of the fifty letters and of their deities: the verse "tadrūpa." My other fifty mūrtis, beginning with Aja, are "differentiations of those forms" — differentiations of the forms of those deities of the eight syllables, Viśva and the rest. By elimination, the sense is that they are manifested out of them. They are "expressible by the fifty letters" in order; and the fifty letters are "differentiated by the syllables of the tāra" — that is, manifested out of the syllables of the praṇava. (5)
Chapter 1 · verse 6
1.6
द्विरष्टपञ्चकचतुःपञ्चेत्येवाष्टवर्गगाः।
अज आनन्द इन्द्रेशावुग्र ऊर्ज ऋतम्भरः॥ ६॥
dviraṣṭapañcakacatuḥpañcetyevāṣṭavargagāḥ|
aja ānanda indreśāvugra ūrja ṛtambharaḥ||
6||
Exact English meaningTwice-eight, a pentad, four, and five — thus the letters stand in exactly eight groups. (Their deities begin:) Aja (a), Ānanda (ā), Indra (i) and Īśa (ī), Ugra (u), Ūrja (ū), Ṛtambhara (ṛ),
Word and compound explanation
"How is that?" — to this he replies with the verse "dviraṣṭa." With the twice-eight, that is, the sixteen vowels, one group is formed; with the stops, a pentad of groups; with the four semivowels, one; with the five sibilants (ūṣmans) taken together with ḷa, one — thus exactly eight groups; and these letters are manifested from the eight syllables of the praṇava. He then names the fifty mūrtis, beginning with "Aja": He is Aja because He is never born. Ānanda, because His very form is bliss. Indra, because He lords over (indati) all that all beings desire — from the root "idi" in the sense of supreme sovereignty. Īśa, because of His rulership; or else "Ī" is Lakṣmī, and He is her Lord. Ugra, in His form as Nṛsiṁha; or else Ugra because at the great dissolution He swallows (grasati) "U," that is, Rudra. Ūrja, because of His strength and because He gives life — from the root "ūrj" meaning strength and vital breath. Ṛtambhara, because He upholds ṛta, truth. (6)
Chapter 1 · verse 7
1.7
ॠघलृशौ लॄजिरेकात्मैर ओजोभृदौरसः।
अन्तोऽर्धगर्भः कपिलः खपतिर्गरुडासनः॥ ७॥
ṝghalṛśau laॄjirekātmaira ojobhṛdaurasaḥ|
anto’rdhagarbhaḥ kapilaḥ khapatirgaruḍāsanaḥ||
7||
Exact English meaningṜgha (ṝ) and Lṛśa (lṛ), Lṝjira (lṝ), Ekātmā (e), Aira (ai), Ojobhṛt (o), Aurasa (au), Anta (aṁ), Ardhagarbha (aḥ), Kapila (ka), Khapati (kha), Garuḍāsana (ga),
Word and compound explanation
"Ṛddha" — so called by reason of all His powers. Some, however, read "Ṝgha"; in that case, He who pervades (iyarti) all, or who knows all, and who shines forth (jigharti), who blazes — hence Ṝgha, derived from the pair of roots ṛ "to go" and ghṛ "to trickle, to shine." Lṛśa: He who takes (lāti), who goes forth (iyarti), and who brings to an end (syati) — hence Lṛśa, from the three roots lā "to take," ṛ "to go," and śo "to whittle down." Lṝjira: He who takes, reaches, conquers, and gives delight — all of this. Ekātmā: eka means "chief"; or the Ātman who is other than the perishable and the imperishable is the Ekātmā — per the lexicon, "eka means chief, other, and sole." Aira: because He is the impeller from every side — from īr "to impel." Ojobhṛt: He who bears ojas, might. Aurasa: because He dwells in the breast (uras) — in the heart — of all souls. Anta: because He brings about the end. Ardhagarbha: "ardha" from ṛdhyati, "he abounds" — He whose womb (interior) is boundless: Mārkaṇḍeya, who entered the belly of Him who lay upon the banyan leaf, drawn in along the path of His breath, ( ) — such is the Mahābhārata account: wandering (within that belly) for a full hundred years, he came to no end of it. Or else "ardha" is Brahmā, since among all souls he most abounds (ṛddha) in qualities: He in whose womb that Brahmā rests (is Ardhagarbha). "Kapila," from His (tawny) color; or else Kapila because He takes (lāti) — accepts as His own — the monkeys (kapi), Hanumat, Sugrīva and the rest. "Khapati": the Lord of kha, the sky, or of perfect bliss. As in the Bhāgavata: "He gives, as it were, the sky — the path of the good — which (He upholds) by (His) ordinance" (text as printed; the citation is elliptical). "Garuḍāsana": He whose seat is Garuḍa. (7)
Chapter 1 · verse 8
1.8
घर्मो ङसारश्चार्वङ्गश्छन्दोगम्यो जनार्दनः।
झाटितारिर्ञमष्टङ्की ठलको डरको ढरी॥ ८॥
gharmo ṅasāraścārvaṅgaśchandogamyo janārdanaḥ|
jhāṭitārirñamaṣṭaṅkī ṭhalako ḍarako ḍharī||
8||
Exact English meaningGharma (gha), Ṅasāra (ṅa), Cārvaṅga (ca), Chandogamya (cha), Janārdana (ja), Jhāṭitāri (jha), Ñama (ña), Ṭaṅkī (ṭa), Ṭhakala (ṭha), Ḍaraka (ḍa), Ḍharī (ḍha),
Word and compound explanation
"Gharma," because His form is blazing splendor — from the root ghṛ, "to trickle, to shine." "Ṅasāra": because He is the essence (sāra) of all, and because He is expressible even by the letter ṅa, He is the essence even of ṅa. "Cārvaṅga": He whose limbs are beautiful — endowed with the loveliness of ten million Kandarpas. "Chandogamya": He who is reached, that is, known, through the chandas, the Vedic texts; as the śruti says, "It is the Vedas indeed that make Him known." "Janārdana": He who drives (ardayati) the janas, the souls — who sends them through saṁsāra; or else Janārdana because He is entreated (adyate), begged, by good people — from the root ard in the senses of going and begging. "Jhāṭitāri": He by whom the enemies are jhāṭita, put to rout. "Ñama": He restrains (yamayati), hence "Yama"; and by the pṛṣodarādi rule the ya becomes ña. "Ṭaṅkī": He who bears the ṭaṅka, the weapon that splits stone. "Ṭhakala": ṭha means the void; (He fills it) with His kalās — through the body composed of the sixteen kalās; per the lexicon, "ṭha means the void and the deep of night." "Ḍaraka": because He soars (ḍayate) in the form of the haṁsa, and because He delights (ramate), being of the nature of joy. "Ḍharī": from ḍhṛ "to flow away" — He who at the dissolution makes all things pour away. (8)
Chapter 1 · verse 9
1.9
णात्मा तारस्थपो दण्डी धन्वी नम्यः परः फली।
बली भगो मनुर्यज्ञो रामो लक्ष्मीपतिर्वरः॥ ९॥
ṇātmā tārasthapo daṇḍī dhanvī namyaḥ paraḥ phalī|
balī bhago manuryajño rāmo lakṣmīpatirvaraḥ||
9||
Exact English meaningṆātmā (ṇa), Tāra (ta), Thabha (tha), Daṇḍī (da), Dhanvī (dha), Namya (na), Para (pa), Phalī (pha), Balī (ba), Bhaga (bha), Manu (ma), Yajña (ya), Rāma (ra), Lakṣmīpati (la), Vara (va),
Word and compound explanation
"Ṇātmā": being the Self of all, He is the Ātmā even of (the letter) ṇa. "Tāra": because He is the deliverer (tāraka), and because His form is the praṇava (tāra). "Thabha": He of steady radiance. "Daṇḍī": He who chastises (daṇḍayati) — disciplines — the gods; or He whose staff (daṇḍa) is the herdsman's rod in His sport of tending the cows and the like; or Daṇḍī from His bearing (the staff) of the renunciate order. "Dhanvī": He whose bow is the excellent, gracious one named Śārṅga. "Namya": worthy of homage from Brahmā and the rest. "Para": protector, fulfiller, supreme — or He who is distinct from both the perishable and the imperishable — from the root pṝ, meaning protecting and filling. "Phalī": the bestower of the fruits of all actions. "Balī": of infinite strength; as the Bhāgavata says, "He indeed is the strength of the strong, and of the others (too)." "Bhaga": He whose form is lordship and the rest; as the Vaiṣṇava (purāṇa) says: "Of lordship entire, of valor, of fame, of splendor, and of knowledge and discriminating wisdom — of these six, 'bhaga' is the term." "Manu," because His very form is knowledge. "Yajña": because He is worshipped (ijyate) — adored — by all. "Rāma": because He delights (ramayati), gladdens, the gods and the others. "Lakṣmīpati": the Lord of Lakṣmī. "Vara": because He is chosen (vriyate) by all. (9)
Chapter 1 · verse 10
1.10
शान्तसंवित् षड्गुणश्च सारात्मा हंसळाळुकौ।
पञ्चाशन्मूर्तयस्त्वेता ममाकारादिलक्षकाः॥ १०॥
śāntasaṃvit ṣaḍguṇaśca sārātmā haṃsaḷāḷukau|
pañcāśanmūrtayastvetā mamākārādilakṣakāḥ||
10||
Exact English meaningŚāntasaṁvit (śa), Ṣaḍguṇa (ṣa), Sārātmā (sa), and the pair Haṁsa (ha) and Ḷāḷuka (ḷa) — these, then, are My fifty mūrtis, indicated by the letters beginning with a.
Word and compound explanation
"Śāntasaṁvit": He whose saṁvit — knowledge — is śāntā, blissful in nature. "Ṣaḍguṇa": He whose form is the six attributes, lordship and the rest. "Sārātmā": He whose ātmā, that is, body, is sāra — constituted of supreme consciousness and bliss. "Haṁsa": because He slays (hanti) the people (at the end), or because He is all (of them). "Ḷāḷuka": from lal, "to sport" — because He possesses the playfulness of divine līlā. The grammatical correctness of these names is to be justified by the pṛṣodarādi rule and by the uṇādi suffixes; for fear of prolixity it is not spelled out here. These fifty mūrtis of Mine, Aja and the rest, are "indicated by a and the other letters": a and the rest are their indicators (lakṣaka) — and, since the letters also carry them, their designators (vācaka); the meaning is that they are the deities of the letters a and so on. But of kṣa, which stands amid the fifty-one, the deity is Nṛsiṁha; that Nṛsiṁha is its deity is indicated by this very verse. (10)
Chapter 1 · verse 11
1.11
नारायणाष्टाक्षरश्च ताराष्टाक्षरभेदवान्।
आद्यैस्तारचतुर्वर्णैर्भिन्ना व्याहृतयः क्रमात्॥ ११॥
nārāyaṇāṣṭākṣaraśca tārāṣṭākṣarabhedavān|
ādyaistāracaturvarṇairbhinnā vyāhṛtayaḥ kramāt||
11||
Exact English meaningThe Nārāyaṇa eight-syllable mantra has its differentiation from the eight syllables of the tāra; and by the first four letters of the tāra the vyāhṛtis are differentiated, in order.
Word and compound explanation
He states the manifestation of the Nārāyaṇāṣṭākṣara out of the eight syllables of the praṇava: "nārāyaṇāṣṭākṣaraś ca." Then he states the manifestation of the vyāhṛtis: "ādyaiḥ." By the first four letters of the tāra, the praṇava — akāra, ukāra, makāra and bindu — the three vyāhṛtis, taken severally and jointly, are "differentiated," that is, manifested. (11)
Chapter 1 · verse 12
1.12
अनिरुद्धादिकास्तासां देवता व्युत्क्रमेण वा।
ताश्चतुर्मूर्तयस्त्वेव द्वादशार्णपदोदिताः॥ १२॥
aniruddhādikāstāsāṃ devatā vyutkrameṇa vā|
tāścaturmūrtayastveva dvādaśārṇapadoditāḥ||
12||
Exact English meaningTheir deities are Aniruddha and the rest — or in the reverse order; and those very four mūrtis are proclaimed by the words of the twelve-syllable mantra.
Word and compound explanation
He states the deities of the vyāhṛtis: "aniruddha." Aniruddha, Pradyumna, Saṅkarṣaṇa and Vāsudeva are the deities of those vyāhṛtis — or in the reverse order, beginning with Vāsudeva and ending with Aniruddha. These, Aniruddha and the rest, are "proclaimed by the words" of the twelve-syllable mantra — the sense is that they are the supreme deities expressed by those words. (12)
Chapter 1 · verse 13
1.13
नारायणाष्टाक्षराच्च व्याहृतिभ्यस्तथैव च।
विभेदो द्वादशार्णानां केशवाद्याश्च देवताः॥ १३॥
nārāyaṇāṣṭākṣarācca vyāhṛtibhyastathaiva ca|
vibhedo dvādaśārṇānāṃ keśavādyāśca devatāḥ||
13||
Exact English meaningFrom the Nārāyaṇa eight-syllable mantra, and likewise from the vyāhṛtis, comes the differentiation of the twelve syllables; and their deities are Keśava and the rest.
Word and compound explanation
He states the manifestation of the twelve-syllable mantra: "nārāyaṇa." From the eight syllables of the Nārāyaṇa mantra and from the vyāhṛtis taken severally and jointly comes the "differentiation" — the manifestation — of the twelve syllables; and the deities of those letters are Keśava at the head and Dāmodara at the end. (13)
Chapter 1 · verse 14
1.14
नारायणाष्टाक्षराच्च व्याहृतित्रिगुणात् पुनः।
वेदमाता तु गायत्री द्विगुणा द्वादशाक्षरात्॥ १४॥
nārāyaṇāṣṭākṣarācca vyāhṛtitriguṇāt punaḥ|
vedamātā tu gāyatrī dviguṇā dvādaśākṣarāt||
14||
Exact English meaningAnd from the Nārāyaṇa eight-syllable mantra joined with the threefold vyāhṛti there arises, in turn, the Gāyatrī, Mother of the Vedas — she who is the double of the twelve-syllable mantra.
Word and compound explanation
He declares the manifestation of the Gāyatrī: "nārāyaṇāṣṭākṣarāc ca." Through the vyāhṛti — through its utterance — the Gāyatrī, the mother of the Vedas, of twenty-four syllables, is manifested, being "triguṇā." Of what sort is she? "Double of the twelve-syllable" — possessed of twenty-four syllables. Some, however, say that "triguṇa" means multiplication by three: since the vyāhṛtis together contain eight syllables, the Gāyatrī is born from the mantra of the vyāhṛtis, severally and jointly, as tripled (8 × 3). Others, reading "dviguṇāt" with the ablative ending, say the Gāyatrī is born from the twelve-syllable mantra recited twice over. And further, through its first, second and third padas — again by those laudations, which convey aggregation — the Gāyatrī is born from the triad of mantras as well; this appears to be the real state of the matter. (14)
Chapter 1 · verse 15
1.15
चतुर्विंशन्मूर्तयोऽस्याः कथिता वर्णदेवताः।
तद्भेदः पौरुषं सूक्तं वेदाः पुरुषसूक्तगाः॥ १५॥
caturviṃśanmūrtayo’syāḥ kathitā varṇadevatāḥ|
tadbhedaḥ pauruṣaṃ sūktaṃ vedāḥ puruṣasūktagāḥ||
15||
Exact English meaningThe twenty-four mūrtis have been declared the deities of her letters; her differentiation is the Puruṣa-sūkta, and the Vedas proceed from the Puruṣa-sūkta.
Word and compound explanation
The twenty-four mūrtis, Keśava and the rest, have been declared in order as the deities of the letters of this Gāyatrī. The Puruṣa-sūkta is the "bheda" of that Gāyatrī — bheda meaning that which is differentiated, that is, manifested (out of her). "Puruṣasūktagāḥ": (the Vedas) go forth from the sūkta — are sung forth from it; hence "proceeding from the Puruṣa-sūkta." (15)
Chapter 1 · verse 16
1.16
वैदिकाः सर्वशब्दाश्च तस्मात् सर्वाभिधोऽस्म्यहम्।
पञ्चाशद्वर्णभिन्नाश्च सर्वशब्दा अतोऽपि च॥ १६॥
vaidikāḥ sarvaśabdāśca tasmāt sarvābhidho’smyaham|
pañcāśadvarṇabhinnāśca sarvaśabdā ato’pi ca||
16||
Exact English meaningAnd all the Vedic words (proceed from it); therefore I am the one named by all words. And all words are differentiated out of the fifty letters — for that reason also (I am named by all).
Word and compound explanation
"And all the Vedic words" — that is, they too proceed from the Puruṣa-sūkta. And since all the Vedas are manifested in due order from the praṇava, which declares My plenitude, therefore I alone am "sarvābhidha" — to be denoted, in the primary signification, by every Vedic word. He then supplies a further reason: "pañcāśadvarṇa." Since all words are differentiated by the fifty letters — are manifested from the fifty letters, or are composed of the fifty letters — for this reason too I am named by all: I am the primary referent of every letter, word, sentence, anuvākya and the rest. (16)
Chapter 1 · verse 17
1.17
ऋषिश्च देवतैकोऽहं तारादीनां विशेषतः।
छन्दो मदीया गायत्री ताराष्टाक्षरयोर्मता॥ १७॥
ṛṣiśca devataiko’haṃ tārādīnāṃ viśeṣataḥ|
chando madīyā gāyatrī tārāṣṭākṣarayormatā||
17||
Exact English meaningAnd I alone am both ṛṣi and deity — of the tāra and the rest in particular; and My own Gāyatrī is held to be the metre of the tāra and of the eight-syllable mantra.
Word and compound explanation
He states the ṛṣi and the other (accessories) of the praṇava and the rest: "ṛṣiś ca." Of the mantras beginning with the tāra, I, the Inner Ruler, am the ṛṣi; and I Myself, the Supreme Self, am also the deity. Of the two mantras — the praṇava and the aṣṭākṣara — My own Gāyatrī, the divine Gāyatrī, is the metre. (17)
Chapter 1 · verses 18–21
1.18
उद्यद्भास्वत्समाभासश्चिदानन्दैकदेहवान्।
चक्रशङ्खगदापद्मधरो ध्येयोऽहमीश्वरः॥ १८॥
udyadbhāsvatsamābhāsaścidānandaikadehavān|
cakraśaṅkhagadāpadmadharo dhyeyo’hamīśvaraḥ||
18||
1.19
लक्ष्मीधराभ्यामाश्लिष्टः स्वमूर्तिगणमध्यगः॥ १९॥
lakṣmīdharābhyāmāśliṣṭaḥ svamūrtigaṇamadhyagaḥ||
19||
1.20
ब्रह्मवायुशिवाहीशविपैः शक्रादिकैरपि।
सेव्यमानोऽधिकं भक्त्या नित्यनिःशेषशक्तिमान्॥ २०॥
brahmavāyuśivāhīśavipaiḥ śakrādikairapi|
sevyamāno’dhikaṃ bhaktyā nityaniḥśeṣaśaktimān||
20||
1.21
मूर्तयोऽष्टावपि ध्येयाश्चक्रशङ्खवराभयैः।
युक्ताः प्रदीपवर्णाश्च सर्वाभरणभूषिताः॥ २१॥
mūrtayo’ṣṭāvapi dhyeyāścakraśaṅkhavarābhayaiḥ|
yuktāḥ pradīpavarṇāśca sarvābharaṇabhūṣitāḥ||
21||
Exact combined meaningI, the Lord, am to be meditated upon: lustrous as the rising sun, having a body of pure consciousness-and-bliss, bearing discus, conch, mace and lotus; embraced by Lakṣmī and Dharā, standing amid the host of My own forms, (served) by Brahmā, Vāyu, Śiva, the Lord of Serpents and the Lord of Birds, and by Śakra and the rest as well, — served with surpassing devotion — I who eternally possess all powers entire. The eight mūrtis too are to be meditated upon, joined with discus, conch, boon and fearlessness, lamp-flame-hued, adorned with every ornament.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
He states the meditation of the praṇava and the aṣṭākṣara: "udyat." Of the color of the rising sun; His form composed of consciousness and bliss; holding, in the order beginning with the upper right hand, the discus, conch, mace and lotus; embraced by Lakṣmī and Dharā stationed at His left and right sides; standing amid the host of His own forms — Kruddholka and the rest, Viśva and the rest, Keśava and the rest, and Matsya and the rest — which form, in order, the first and the succeeding enclosures (āvaraṇas): Nārāyaṇa stands at the center. Served — in the highest measure, with devotion — by Brahmā, Vāyu and Śiva, by the Lord of Serpents, Śeṣa, and by Vipa, that is, Garuḍa; and, outside of them, by Śakra and the other world-guardians, each abiding in his own quarter. "Eternally possessed of all powers entire": He whose powers are eternal — unfailing — and entire, that is, complete. The eight mūrtis also — Ātmā and the rest, the embodied deities of the (eight) syllables — are to be meditated upon: joined with discus, conch, boon and fearlessness; having the color of a lamp-flame; adorned with all ornaments. (18–20)
Chapter 1 · verse 22
1.22
तादृग्रूपाश्च पञ्चाशज्ज्ञानमुद्राभयोद्यताः।
टङ्की दण्डी च धन्वी च तत्तद्युक्तास्तु वामतः॥ २२॥
tādṛgrūpāśca pañcāśajjñānamudrābhayodyatāḥ|
ṭaṅkī daṇḍī ca dhanvī ca tattadyuktāstu vāmataḥ||
22||
Exact English meaningAnd the fifty, of like form, are raised in the jñāna-mudrā and the gesture of fearlessness; but Ṭaṅkī, Daṇḍī and Dhanvī bear each his own emblem on the left.
Word and compound explanation
He states the meditation of the deities of the mātṛkā-mantra: "tādṛgrūpāś ca." The fifty, Aja and the rest, are of like form — lamp-flame-hued — their two hands joined with the jñāna-mudrā and the abhaya-mudrā. But Ṭaṅkī, Daṇḍī and Dhanvī are to be meditated upon as bearing on the left — in the left hand — the ṭaṅka and their other (emblems). Some hold that the mātṛkā deities here are simply two-armed, because of the words "raised in jñāna-mudrā and fearlessness." Others say that the mention of jñāna-mudrā and abhaya stands in the place of boon and fearlessness, while their upper arms joined with discus and conch were already stated before. In this matter the mode of practice may vary according to the tradition of one's own guru. (21)
Chapter 1 · verse 23
1.23
वासुदेवादिकाः शुक्लरक्तपीतासितोज्ज्वलाः।
चक्रशङ्खगदाब्जेतः प्रथमो मुसली हली॥ २३॥
vāsudevādikāḥ śuklaraktapītāsitojjvalāḥ|
cakraśaṅkhagadābjetaḥ prathamo musalī halī||
23||
Exact English meaningVāsudeva and the rest are white, red, yellow, and gleaming dark; the first is endowed with discus, conch, mace and lotus; (the next is) the bearer of pestle and plough.
Word and compound explanation
He states the meditation of the deities of the vyāhṛtis, Vāsudeva and the rest: "vāsudevādikāḥ." Vāsudeva is white; ( ) Saṅkarṣaṇa is red; Pradyumna, yellow; Aniruddha, gleaming dark. Among them the first, Vāsudeva, is "endowed with discus, conch, mace and lotus" — his four arms joined with the discus and the rest. The next, Saṅkarṣaṇa, is the pestle-bearer and plough-bearer: he carries the pestle and the plough with his lower left and right arms in that order, while in his two upper arms he holds the conch and the discus. (22)
Chapter 1 · verse 24
1.24
सशङ्खचक्रस्त्वपरस्तृतीयः शार्ङ्गबाणवान्।
सचक्रशङ्खस्तुर्यस्तु चक्रशङ्खासिचर्मवान्॥ २४॥
saśaṅkhacakrastvaparastṛtīyaḥ śārṅgabāṇavān|
sacakraśaṅkhasturyastu cakraśaṅkhāsicarmavān||
24||
Exact English meaningThe next (Saṅkarṣaṇa) holds conch and discus (above); the third bears the Śārṅga bow and arrow, with conch and discus (above); but the fourth carries discus, conch, sword and shield.
Word and compound explanation
The third, Pradyumna, bears the Śārṅga bow and the arrow in his two lower hands, and in his two upper arms he holds the conch and the discus. The fourth (turya), Aniruddha, beginning with the upper right arm, bears in order the discus, conch, sword and shield. (23)
Chapter 1 · verse 25
1.25
केशवो मधुसूदनः सङ्कर्षणदामोदरौ।
सवासुदेवः प्रद्युम्नो दक्षोच्चकरशङ्खिनः॥ २५॥
keśavo madhusūdanaḥ saṅkarṣaṇadāmodarau|
savāsudevaḥ pradyumno dakṣoccakaraśaṅkhinaḥ||
25||
Exact English meaningKeśava, Madhusūdana, Saṅkarṣaṇa and Dāmodara, together with Vāsudeva and Pradyumna, hold the conch in the upper right hand.
Word and compound explanation
He states the distinguishing marks — by discus and the other emblems — of Keśava and the rest, the deities of the letters of the Gāyatrī, in the verses beginning "keśava." The six beginning with Keśava bear the conch in the upper right hand. (24)
Chapter 1 · verses 26–28
1.26
विष्णुमाधवानिरुद्धपुरुषोत्तमाधोक्षजाः।
जनार्दनश्च वामोच्चकरस्थितदरा मताः॥ २६॥
viṣṇumādhavāniruddhapuruṣottamādhokṣajāḥ|
janārdanaśca vāmoccakarasthitadarā matāḥ||
26||
1.27
गोविन्दश्च त्रिविक्रमः सश्रीधरहृषीकपः।
नृसिंहश्चाच्युतश्चैव वामाधःकरशङ्खिनः॥ २७॥
govindaśca trivikramaḥ saśrīdharahṛṣīkapaḥ|
nṛsiṃhaścācyutaścaiva vāmādhaḥkaraśaṅkhinaḥ||

1.28
वामनः सनारायणः पद्मनाभ उपेन्द्रकः।
हरिः कृष्णश्च दक्षाधःकरशङ्खधरा मताः॥ २८॥
vāmanaḥ sanārāyaṇaḥ padmanābha upendrakaḥ|
hariḥ kṛṣṇaśca dakṣādhaḥkaraśaṅkhadharā matāḥ||
28||
Exact combined meaningViṣṇu, Mādhava, Aniruddha, Puruṣottama, Adhokṣaja and Janārdana are held to have the conch stationed in the upper left hand; Govinda, Trivikrama, together with Śrīdhara and Hṛṣīkeśa, Nṛsiṁha and Acyuta bear the conch in the lower left hand; Vāmana together with Nārāyaṇa, Padmanābha, Upendra, Hari and Kṛṣṇa are held to be bearers of the conch in the lower right hand.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
The six beginning with Viṣṇu have the conch stationed in the upper left hand. The six beginning with Govinda are joined with the conch in the lower left hand. The six beginning with Vāmana bear the conch in the lower right arm. (Memorial verse:) "śā-ca-gau-pī; śa-paṁ-go-caṁ; śa-paṁ-cā-gaṁ; śa-gaṁ-ca-paṁ; śā-ca-pa-gaṁ; śa-gaṁ-pa-caṁ — of the bearers, in the order of hands beginning from the upper" (śa = conch, ca = discus, ga = mace, pa = lotus). (25–27)
Chapter 1 · verses 29–40
1.29
शङ्खचक्रगदापद्मधराश्चैते हि सर्वशः।
क्रमव्युत्क्रमपद्मादिगदादिव्युत्क्रमस्तथा॥ २९॥
śaṅkhacakragadāpadmadharāścaite hi sarvaśaḥ|
kramavyutkramapadmādigadādivyutkramastathā||
29||
1.30
अर्धक्रमः सान्तरश्च षट्सुषट्स्वरिपूर्विणाम्।
वर्णानां देवतानां च नित्यत्वान्न क्रमः स्वतः॥ ३०॥
ardhakramaḥ sāntaraśca ṣaṭsuṣaṭsvaripūrviṇām|
varṇānāṃ devatānāṃ ca nityatvānna kramaḥ svataḥ||
30||
1.31
व्यक्तिक्रमं ब्रह्मबुद्धावपेक्ष्य क्रम उच्यते।
समासव्यासयोगेन व्याहृतीनां चतुष्टयम्॥ ३१॥
vyaktikramaṃ brahmabuddhāvapekṣya krama ucyate|
samāsavyāsayogena vyāhṛtīnāṃ catuṣṭayam||
31||
1.32
सत्यं चाङ्गानि तारस्य प्रोच्यन्तेऽष्टाक्षरस्य च।
कृद्धमहावीरद्युसहस्रसहितोल्ककाः॥ ३२॥
satyaṃ cāṅgāni tārasya procyante’ṣṭākṣarasya ca|
kṛddhamahāvīradyusahasrasahitolkakāḥ||
32||
1.33
चतुर्थ्यन्ता हृदादीनि पृथग्रूपाणि तानि च।
विष्णोरेवात्यभेदेऽपि तदैश्वर्यात् तदन्यवत्॥ ३३॥
caturthyantā hṛdādīni pṛthagrūpāṇi tāni ca|
viṣṇorevātyabhede’pi tadaiśvaryāt tadanyavat||
33||
1.34
चक्रशङ्खवराभीतिहस्तान्येतानि सर्वशः।
मूलरूपसवर्णानि कृष्णवर्णा शिखोच्यते॥ ३४॥
cakraśaṅkhavarābhītihastānyetāni sarvaśaḥ|
mūlarūpasavarṇāni kṛṣṇavarṇā śikhocyate||
34||
1.35
चतुर्विंशन्मूर्तयश्च मूलरूपसवर्णकाः।
आदिवर्णत्रयं नाभिहृच्छिरस्सु यथाक्रमम्॥ ३५॥
caturviṃśanmūrtayaśca mūlarūpasavarṇakāḥ|
ādivarṇatrayaṃ nābhihṛcchirassu yathākramam||
35||
1.36
न्यसनीयं च तद्वर्णदेवताध्यानपूर्वकम्।
पज्जानुनाभिहृदयवाङ्नासानेत्रकेषु च॥ ३६॥
nyasanīyaṃ ca tadvarṇadevatādhyānapūrvakam|
pajjānunābhihṛdayavāṅnāsānetrakeṣu ca||
36||
1.37
अष्टाक्षराणां न्यासः स्याद् व्याहृतीनां प्रजापतिः।
मुनिश्छन्दस्तु गायत्री देवता भगवान् हरिः॥ ३७॥
aṣṭākṣarāṇāṃ nyāsaḥ syād vyāhṛtīnāṃ prajāpatiḥ|
muniśchandastu gāyatrī devatā bhagavān hariḥ||
37||
1.38
उद्यदादित्यवर्णश्च ज्ञानमुद्राभयोद्यतः।
तारेण व्याहृतीभिश्च ज्ञेयान्यङ्गानि पञ्च च॥ ३८॥
udyadādityavarṇaśca jñānamudrābhayodyataḥ|
tāreṇa vyāhṛtībhiśca jñeyānyaṅgāni pañca ca||
38||
1.39
नाभिहृत्केषु सर्वेषु चतस्रो व्याहृतीर्न्यसेत्।
द्वादशार्णस्य जगतीच्छन्दोऽन्यत् तारवत् स्मृतम्॥ ३९॥
nābhihṛtkeṣu sarveṣu catasro vyāhṛtīrnyaset|
dvādaśārṇasya jagatīcchando’nyat tāravat smṛtam||
39||
1.40
अच्छवर्णोऽभयवरकरो ध्येयोऽमितद्युतिः।
पदैर्व्यस्तैः समस्तैश्च ज्ञेयान्यङ्गानि पञ्च च।
अष्टाक्षराणां स्थानेषु बाह्वोरूर्वोश्च विन्यसेत्॥ ४०॥
acchavarṇo’bhayavarakaro dhyeyo’mitadyutiḥ|
padairvyastaiḥ samastaiśca jñeyānyaṅgāni pañca ca|
aṣṭākṣarāṇāṃ sthāneṣu bāhvorūrvośca vinyaset||
40||
Exact combined meaningFor these all, in every case, are bearers of conch, discus, mace and lotus: (the arrangements are) the direct order, the reverse order, the order beginning with the lotus, the reverse beginning with the mace, the half-order, and the alternating one — among the six sets of six, for those preceded by the discus. Since the letters and the deities are eternal, no order belongs to them of themselves; the order is spoken of with reference to the sequence of their manifestation in Brahmā's understanding. By way of combination and separation the vyāhṛtis make four, with Satya (as the fifth); these are declared the limbs (aṅgas) of the tāra and of the eight-syllable mantra. The Ulkās — Kṛddha, Mahā, Vīra, Dyulka, together with Sahasra — with fourth-case (dative) endings, are (the mantras of) the heart and the rest; and they are His separate forms. Though they are absolutely non-different from Viṣṇu Himself, by His lordly power they appear as if other than Him. All these (Ulkā forms) have their hands in discus, conch, boon and fearlessness, and are of the same color as the root form; but the śikhā (Vīrolka) is said to be black-hued. And the twenty-four mūrtis are of the same color as the root form. The first three letters are to be placed (in nyāsa) on the navel, the heart and the head in due order, preceded by meditation on the deities of those letters. On the feet, knees, navel, heart, mouth, nose, eyes and head is the nyāsa of the eight syllables. Of the vyāhṛtis, Prajāpati is the sage (muni), the metre is Gāyatrī, and the deity is the Lord Hari — of the color of the rising sun, raised in the jñāna-mudrā and the gesture of fearlessness. With the tāra and the vyāhṛtis the five limbs are to be known; and on the navel, the heart, the head, and on all (the limbs), one should place the four vyāhṛtis. Of the twelve-syllable mantra the metre is Jagatī; the rest is held to be as for the tāra. (Its deity) is to be meditated upon as of clear (white) color, His hands in fearlessness and boon, of measureless splendor. By its words, taken severally and jointly, the five limbs are to be known; and one should place (its letters) upon the stations of the eight syllables and upon the arms and the thighs.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
These, Keśava and the rest, are bearers of conch, discus, mace and lotus — "sarvaśaḥ," every one of them. As for "kramavyutkrama —": "of those preceded by the ari": those in which the ari, the discus, comes first are "aripūrvin" — the three (emblems) beginning with the discus; of these ( Since, for the six sets of six beginning with Keśava, the conch's place has been stated as fixed, the six arrangements of the remaining three — discus and the rest — through the difference of krama and the other orders, are to be easily understood, in sequence, for the six (sets) beginning with Keśava. "Discus, mace, lotus" is the krama (direct order); "lotus, mace, discus" is the vyutkrama (reverse order); "lotus, discus, mace" is the padma-krama; "mace, discus, lotus" is the gadā-vyutkrama; "discus, lotus, mace" is the ardha-krama (half-order); "mace, lotus, discus" is the sāntara-krama (alternating order). Here are the summary verses (śa = conch, ca = discus, ga = mace, pa = lotus, dara/jalaja = conch, Kaumodakī = mace, Sudarśana = discus): (1) "Keśava is with śa-ca-ga-pa; Nārāyaṇa with pa-ga-ca and śa; then Mādhava too with ca-śā-pa-ga; Govinda with ga-pa-śa-ca; Viṣṇu with pa-śa-ca and ga; and the Slayer of Madhu with śa-pa-ga-ca. (2) Trivikrama with ga-ca-śa-pa; Vāmana is with ca-ga, lotus and conch; the Lotus-bearer (Śrīdhara) too is proclaimed with ca-ga-śa-pa(?); and Hṛṣīkeśa with ca-pa-śa-ga; Padmanābha here with pa, with ca-ga, and with śa. (3) Dāmodara with śa-ga-ca-pa; Saṅkarṣaṇa with śa-pa, discus and ga; then Vāsudeva with śa-ca-pa-ga; Pradyumna with śa-ga-pa-ca; the form of Aniruddha is known by mace, śa-pa and discus; Puruṣottama bore (his emblems) next. (4) By the arms holding pa-śa-ga-ca Adhokṣaja is known; then here the Man-lion-faced (Nṛsiṁha) with mace, conch, discus and lotus; and Acyuta is declared bearing with his arms the lotus, the mace, the conch (jalaja) and the fair Sudarśana — pa-ca-śa-ga. (5) Janārdana is stated here with ca-śa-ga-pa; Upendra is proclaimed with Kaumodakī (mace), discus, lotus and conch; Hari is stated with ca-pa-ga-śa; Kṛṣṇa with ga-pa-ca and śa. This order here begins from the upper right arm." (28) "But since the mantras, the praṇava and the rest, and their deities are eternal, how can there be the order stated above?" Anticipating this doubt he says: "varṇānām." That which was manifested first in Brahmā's understanding is first; that which followed it is second — thus the statement of order is justified precisely by the order of manifestation. (29) He states the limbs of the praṇava and the rest: "samāsa." "Bhūr bhuvaḥ svaḥ" as one compound, and the three taken severally (make the four); the ritual usage is: "Oṁ bhūḥ — to the heart, whose nature is Vāsudeva, obeisance (namaḥ)," and so forth. (30) But of the eight-syllable mantra the five limbs are Kṛddholka, Maholka, Vīrolka, Dyulka and Sahasrolka, with dative endings; and these, Kṛddholka and the rest, he declares, are Viṣṇu's own forms: "pṛthak" — the sense is that they are separate forms of Viṣṇu Himself. (31) "Atyabhede" — even though there is absolute non-difference, they shine forth as if other than Him. The reason for this: "by His lordship" — by the lordly power of that very Viṣṇu, that is, by His inherent capability. Or else (the sense is): although all of them have the color of the rising sun, the śikhā — "śikhā" meaning the one standing in the śikhā's place, Vīrolka — (is black); indeed the locks of His hair are black. He states the color of Keśava and the rest: "caturviṁśat" — they have the same color as the root form; the meaning is that they shine like the rising sun. (32–33) He states the nyāsa of the letters of the praṇava-mantra: "ādivarṇatrayam." The three letters beginning with a: the deity of the letter a is Viśva; of the letter u, Taijasa; of the letter ma, Prājña. "On the navel, heart and head" is said with the collective nyāsa in view. The ritual usage is "Oṁ aṁ viśvāya namaḥ," and so on — for, according to another āgama, "one should place the letters together with the bindu." (34) He states the nyāsa of the letters of the eight-syllable mantra: "pajjānu." The places are stated in the order of dissolution (from the feet upward); the order of creation, be it known, begins from the head. (The usage:) "Oṁ aṁ viśvātmane namaḥ — on the head," and so forth. He states the ṛṣi and the rest of the vyāhṛti-mantra: "vyāhṛtīnām." (35) He states the meditation on the deity of the vyāhṛtis: "udyat." In the two lower arms are the jñāna-mudrā and the abhaya-mudrā; in the two upper arms, the discus and the conch alone — four-armedness is to be contemplated everywhere. Some, however, say that since only the pair jñāna-mudrā and abhaya is mentioned, He is to be meditated as two-armed only. (36) He states the five limbs of the vyāhṛti-mantra: "tāreṇa." "With the vyāhṛtis" — with the four, taken severally and jointly. He states a further nyāsa: "nābhi." On the navel, the heart and the head, and on all the limbs, one should place the four vyāhṛtis. Here too the usage is to be known as "Oṁ bhūḥ vāsudevātmane namaḥ — on the navel," and so on. (37) He states the five limbs: "padaiḥ." By the four words of the mantra, beginning with the praṇava, and by the whole of it, are the five limbs. The nyāsa is to be performed together with the deities of the words, since it is enjoined as "preceded by meditation on the deity." The usage: "Oṁ, to the heart of the nature of Vāsudeva, namaḥ," and so on. He states the letter-nyāsa: "aṣṭākṣarāṇām." On the stations — the feet, knees and so forth — and on the arms and thighs one should place the letters together with their gods. The usage, in that order: "Oṁ oṁ, to Keśava namaḥ — on the head; Oṁ naṁ, to Nārāyaṇa namaḥ," and so forth. (38–39)
Chapter 1 · verse 41
1.41
विश्वामित्रस्तु सन्ध्यार्थे तदन्यत्र प्रजापतिः।
मुनिर्देवस्तु सवितृनामा स्रष्टृत्वतो हरिः॥ ४१॥
viśvāmitrastu sandhyārthe tadanyatra prajāpatiḥ|
munirdevastu savitṛnāmā sraṣṭṛtvato hariḥ||
41||
Exact English meaningFor the sandhyā, however, (the ṛṣi) is Viśvāmitra; elsewhere the muni is Prajāpati. And the god is he who bears the name Savitṛ — Hari, by reason of His creatorship.
Word and compound explanation
He states the ṛṣi and the rest of the Gāyatrī: "viśvāmitras tu." For the purpose of the sandhyā — for the Gāyatrī that is a limb of the sandhyā worship — the ṛṣi is Viśvāmitra; elsewhere it is Brahmā. The god is Hari named Savitṛ, by reason of His being the creator. (40)
Chapter 1 · verse 42
1.42
प्रोद्यदादित्यवर्णश्च सूर्यमण्डलमध्यगः।
चक्रशङ्खधरोऽङ्कस्थदोर्द्वयो ध्येय एव च॥ ४२॥
prodyadādityavarṇaśca sūryamaṇḍalamadhyagaḥ|
cakraśaṅkhadharo’ṅkasthadordvayo dhyeya eva ca||
42||
Exact English meaningHe is to be meditated upon as having the color of the fully-risen sun, standing within the orb of the sun, holding discus and conch, His two (lower) arms resting upon His lap.
Word and compound explanation
He states the meditation: "prodyat." With his two upper arms he holds the conch and the discus; his two lower hands rest upon his lap. (41)
Chapter 1 · verse 43
1.43
सताराश्च व्याहृतयो गायत्र्यङ्गानि पञ्च च।
दोः पत्सन्धिषु साग्रेषु नाभिहृन्मुखकेषु च॥ ४३॥
satārāśca vyāhṛtayo gāyatryaṅgāni pañca ca|
doḥ patsandhiṣu sāgreṣu nābhihṛnmukhakeṣu ca||
43||
Exact English meaningThe vyāhṛtis together with the tāra are the five limbs of the Gāyatrī; (her letters are placed) on the joints of the arms and legs together with their tips, and on the navel, heart, mouth and head.
Word and compound explanation
He states the five limbs: "satārāś ca." The usage is as with the limbs of the vyāhṛti-mantra. He states the letter-nyāsa: "on the joints of arms and feet together with the tips." On the joints of the arms and legs together with their tips — five (points on each limb) — go twenty letters; four one should place on the navel, heart, mouth and head — such is the meaning. Here too accompaniment by Keśava and the other letter-deities is to be understood. (42)
Chapter 1 · verse 44
1.44
वर्णन्यासश्च कर्तव्यस्तारवन्निखिलं स्मृतम्।
पञ्चाशदक्षराणां च पुंसूक्तस्यापि सर्वशः॥ ४४॥
varṇanyāsaśca kartavyastāravannikhilaṃ smṛtam|
pañcāśadakṣarāṇāṃ ca puṃsūktasyāpi sarvaśaḥ||
44||
Exact English meaningAnd the letter-nyāsa is to be performed; everything (else) is held to be as with the tāra — for the fifty letters, and for the Puruṣa-sūkta too, in every respect.
Word and compound explanation
He states the ṛṣi and the rest of the mātṛkā and of the Puruṣa-sūkta: "tāravat." Of the fifty letters, and of the Puruṣa-sūkta as well, the metre is Anuṣṭubh; the ṛṣi and the deity are as for the tāra — the very same as of the praṇava-mantra. (43)
Chapter 1 · verse 45
1.45
अनुष्टुभश्च त्रिष्टुप् च छन्दोऽस्य त्रिष्टुभोऽपि वा।
विष्णुशब्दश्चतुर्थ्यन्तो हृदयेतः षडक्षरः॥ ४५॥
anuṣṭubhaśca triṣṭup ca chando’sya triṣṭubho’pi vā|
viṣṇuśabdaścaturthyanto hṛdayetaḥ ṣaḍakṣaraḥ||
45||
Exact English meaningIts metres are Anuṣṭubh and Triṣṭubh — or rather the Triṣṭubhs (in the plural); the word 'Viṣṇu' with the dative ending, joined with the hṛdaya (namaḥ), is the six-syllable mantra.
Word and compound explanation
Of the final mantra of the Puruṣa-sūkta the metre is Triṣṭubh; the plural "triṣṭubho 'pi vā" is used with the intent that in the Puruṣa-sūkta of the Taittirīyas many mantras are Triṣṭubhs. He states the Viṣṇu-mantra: "viṣṇuśabda." With the word namaḥ as its "heart," and with ṣa and ṇa counted as separate, it has six syllables; even so, "viṣṇave namaḥ" is the form of this mantra, sasrāpatākhavat (this final word is obscure as printed in this edition). (44)
Chapter 1 · verse 46
1.46
तारवत् सर्वमस्यापि श्यामो ध्येयो हरिः स्वयम्।
वर्णा एव षडङ्गानि षणयोर्भेदयोगतः॥ ४६॥
tāravat sarvamasyāpi śyāmo dhyeyo hariḥ svayam|
varṇā eva ṣaḍaṅgāni ṣaṇayorbhedayogataḥ||
46||
Exact English meaningFor this too everything is as with the tāra; Hari Himself, dark-hued, is to be meditated upon; its very letters, with ṣa and ṇa taken separately, constitute the six limbs.
Word and compound explanation
Of this too all — the metre, the ṛṣi and the deities — is as with the tāra. "Śyāma": lustrous like a sapphire. With the letters ṣa and ṇa distinguished, the limb-nyāsa is performed with its six letters. (45)
Chapter 1 · verses 47–48
1.47
पज्जानुनाभिहृन्नासाकेषु न्यासश्च वर्णशः।
एते तु सर्वमन्त्राणां मूलमन्त्रा विशेषतः॥ ४७॥
pajjānunābhihṛnnāsākeṣu nyāsaśca varṇaśaḥ|
ete tu sarvamantrāṇāṃ mūlamantrā viśeṣataḥ||
47||
1.48
एतज्ज्ञानात् समस्तं च ज्ञातं स्याच्छब्दगोचरम्।
एतज्जपात् समस्तानां मन्त्राणां जापको भवेत्॥ ४८॥
etajjñānāt samastaṃ ca jñātaṃ syācchabdagocaram|
etajjapāt samastānāṃ mantrāṇāṃ jāpako bhavet||
48||
Exact combined meaningAnd the nyāsa, letter by letter, is on the feet, knees, navel, heart, nose and head. These, moreover, are in a special sense the root-mantras of all mantras. Through the knowledge of these, everything that lies within the range of words becomes known; through the japa of these one becomes the reciter of all mantras.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
He states the stations of the letter-nyāsa in the collective order: "pajjānu." Here, because of the words "as with the tāra," it is to be borne in mind that Viśva and the rest are the deities of the letters; and accordingly the nyāsa is of the letters as combined with them. The very order in which the limb-nyāsas of the praṇava and the rest have been declared is the order to be observed in japa. Some, however, say that the order of japa follows the order of manifestation itself. Many followers of tradition say that only renunciates are entitled to the japa of the praṇava, and not householders. But others say that householders too are entitled, both because mantras recited together with the praṇava are declared to yield greater fruit, and because no prohibition is (anywhere) heard. — "But since the mantras of the Lord are many, how is it that the limbs and the rest are prescribed for these eight alone?" To this he replies "ete tu": these are the mūla-mantras, the primal mantras; for that reason they are taught first. Others as well will be stated in the fourth chapter. (46–47)

भगवत्पूजाकथनम्

Chapter 1 · verse 49
1.49
पूज्यश्च भगवान् नित्यं चक्राब्जादिकमण्डले।
हृदये वा चले वाऽपि जले वा केवलस्थले॥ ४९॥
pūjyaśca bhagavān nityaṃ cakrābjādikamaṇḍale|
hṛdaye vā cale vā’pi jale vā kevalasthale||
49||
Exact English meaningAnd the Lord is ever to be worshipped — in the cakrābja or other maṇḍala, or in the heart, or in a movable (image), or in water, or on bare ground.
Word and compound explanation
Now he states the rule of worship: "pūjyaś ca." The characteristics of the cakrābja-maṇḍala will be stated (later). By the word "ādi" are meant the Sarvatobhadra and other (maṇḍalas). "In the heart" — with mental services; "in water" — with arghya and the rest, with sandal-paste, flowers and so on; "in the movable" — in a movable image, or in the śālagrāma; "on bare ground" — on the prepared altar-ground (sthaṇḍila). (48)
Chapter 1 · verses 50–51
1.50
अष्टाक्षरेण सम्पूज्य प्रथमं देवतां पराम्।
मध्ये सव्ये गुरूंश्चैव दक्षिणे सर्वदेवताः॥ ५०॥
aṣṭākṣareṇa sampūjya prathamaṃ devatāṃ parām|
madhye savye gurūṃścaiva dakṣiṇe sarvadevatāḥ||
50||
1.51
पुनः सव्ये सर्वगुरूनाग्नेयादिषु च क्रमात्।
गरुडं व्यासदेवं च दुर्गां चैव सरस्वतीम्॥ ५१॥
punaḥ savye sarvagurūnāgneyādiṣu ca kramāt|
garuḍaṃ vyāsadevaṃ ca durgāṃ caiva sarasvatīm||
51||
Exact combined meaningHaving first worshipped with the eight-syllable mantra the supreme Deity in the middle, and the gurus on the left, and all the deities on the right, again on the left all the gurus; and in the south-east and the other corners, in order, Garuḍa, Vyāsadeva, Durgā and Sarasvatī.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
He states the worship of the deities of the seat (pīṭha): "aṣṭākṣareṇa." (49–50)
Chapter 1 · verses 52–55
1.52
धर्मं ज्ञानं च वैराग्यमैश्वर्यं चैव कोणगान्।
तदन्तः पूर्वदिक्पूर्वमधर्मादींश्च पूजयेत्॥ ५२॥
dharmaṃ jñānaṃ ca vairāgyamaiśvaryaṃ caiva koṇagān|
tadantaḥ pūrvadikpūrvamadharmādīṃśca pūjayet||
52||
1.53
अपूजिता अधर्मादिदातारस्ते तथाभिधाः।
निर्ऋतिश्चैव दुर्गा च कामो रुद्रश्च देवताः॥ ५३॥
apūjitā adharmādidātāraste tathābhidhāḥ|
nirṛtiścaiva durgā ca kāmo rudraśca devatāḥ||
53||
1.54
यमवायुशिवेन्द्राश्च ज्ञेया धर्मादिदेवताः।
परमः पुरुषो मध्ये शक्तिराधाररूपिणी॥ ५४॥
yamavāyuśivendrāśca jñeyā dharmādidevatāḥ|
paramaḥ puruṣo madhye śaktirādhārarūpiṇī||
54||
1.55
कूर्मोऽनन्तश्च पृथिवी क्षीरसागर एव च।
श्वेतद्वीपो मण्टपश्च दिव्यरत्नमयो महान्॥ ५५॥
kūrmo’nantaśca pṛthivī kṣīrasāgara eva ca|
śvetadvīpo maṇṭapaśca divyaratnamayo mahān||
55||
Exact combined meaningOne should worship Dharma, Jñāna, Vairāgya and Aiśvarya stationed at the corners, and within them, beginning from the east, Adharma and the rest. Left unworshipped, these become bestowers of adharma and the rest — whence their names; their deities are Nirṛti, Durgā, Kāma and Rudra, while Yama, Vāyu, Śiva and Indra are to be known as the deities of Dharma and the rest. In the middle is the Supreme Person, with Śakti in the form of the support (ādhāra-śakti); (then) Kūrma, Ananta, the Earth, the very Ocean of Milk, Śvetadvīpa, and the great pavilion made of celestial gems.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
"Within them" — in their midst. "Beginning from the east" — in the east and the other quarters. Here many followers of tradition hold that "east" means the direction facing the deity; others say it is the worshipper's east. (51–54)
Chapter 1 · verses 56–58
1.56
पद्ममेतत् त्रयं देवी रमैव बहुरूपिणी।
सूर्यसोमहुताशाश्च पद्मे श्रीस्त्रिगुणात्मिका॥ ५६॥
padmametat trayaṃ devī ramaiva bahurūpiṇī|
sūryasomahutāśāśca padme śrīstriguṇātmikā||
56||
1.57
आत्मान्तरात्मपरमज्ञानात्मानश्च मूर्तयः।
विमलोत्कर्षणी ज्ञाना क्रिया योगा तथैव च॥ ५७॥
ātmāntarātmaparamajñānātmānaśca mūrtayaḥ|
vimalotkarṣaṇī jñānā kriyā yogā tathaiva ca||
57||
1.58
प्रह्वी सत्या तथेशानाऽनुग्रहा चेति शक्तयः।
अष्टदिक्षु च मध्ये च स्वरूपाण्येव ता हरेः॥ ५८॥
prahvī satyā tatheśānā’nugrahā ceti śaktayaḥ|
aṣṭadikṣu ca madhye ca svarūpāṇyeva tā hareḥ||
58||
Exact combined meaning(Then) the lotus — this triad is the Goddess, Ramā herself, of many forms; and on the lotus are Sūrya, Soma and Hutāśa (Fire), and Śrī composed of the three guṇas; and the mūrtis Ātmā, Antarātmā, Paramātmā and Jñānātmā; and the śaktis Vimalā, Utkarṣaṇī, Jñānā, Kriyā, and likewise Yogā, Prahvī, Satyā, then Īśānā and Anugrahā — in the eight quarters and in the center; and they are all forms of Hari's own being.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
"This triad" — the triad of Śvetadvīpa, the jeweled pavilion, and the lotus — is verily a form of Lakṣmī. (55–57)
Chapter 1 · verse 59
1.59
ततोऽनन्तं योगपीठस्वरूपं पूजयेद्धरेः।
तत्रावाह्य हरिं चार्घ्यं पाद्यमाचमनीयकम्॥ ५९॥
tato’nantaṃ yogapīṭhasvarūpaṃ pūjayeddhareḥ|
tatrāvāhya hariṃ cārghyaṃ pādyamācamanīyakam||
59||
Exact English meaningThereafter one should worship Ananta, the true form of Hari's yoga-seat; and having invoked Hari upon it, (one should offer) the arghya, the pādya and the ācamanīya.
Word and compound explanation
"There" — upon the pīṭha thus worshipped — "having invoked" (Hari). (58)
Chapter 1 · verse 60
1.60
मधुपर्कं पुनश्चाचां स्नानं वासो विभूषणम्।
उपवीतासने दत्वा गन्धपुष्पे तथैव च॥ ६०॥
madhuparkaṃ punaścācāṃ snānaṃ vāso vibhūṣaṇam|
upavītāsane datvā gandhapuṣpe tathaiva ca||
60||
Exact English meaning(Then) the madhuparka, and again the ācamanīya; having offered the bath, the garment, the ornaments, the sacred thread and the seat, and likewise sandal-paste and flowers,
Word and compound explanation
"Punaś ca" — once again. "Ācām" means the ācamanīya; or else it is an auṇādika derivative from the root cam(u), "to sip." (59)
Chapter 1 · verse 61
1.61
लक्ष्मीधरे यजेत् तत्र पार्श्वयोरुभयोर्हरेः।
हृदयादींस्तथेन्द्रादिदिक्ष्वस्त्रं कोणकेषु च॥ ६१॥
lakṣmīdhare yajet tatra pārśvayorubhayorhareḥ|
hṛdayādīṃstathendrādidikṣvastraṃ koṇakeṣu ca||
61||
Exact English meaningThere one should worship Lakṣmī and Dharā at both sides of Hari; likewise the heart and the other (limb-mantras) in the quarters of Indra and the rest, and the weapon (astra) in the corners.
Word and compound explanation
He states the worship of the deities of the enclosures (āvaraṇas): "lakṣmīdhare." "The heart and the rest" — Kruddholka and the others; the astra is Sahasrolka. (60)
Chapter 1 · verses 62–63
1.62
वासुदेवादिकान् दिक्षु केशवादींस्ततः परम्।
मत्स्यकूर्मवराहांश्च नारसिंहं च वामनम्॥ ६२॥
vāsudevādikān dikṣu keśavādīṃstataḥ param|
matsyakūrmavarāhāṃśca nārasiṃhaṃ ca vāmanam||
62||
1.63
भार्गवं राघवं कृष्णं बुद्धं कल्किनमेव च।
अनन्तं विश्वरूपं च तद्बहिः पूजयेत् क्रमात्॥ ६३॥
bhārgavaṃ rāghavaṃ kṛṣṇaṃ buddhaṃ kalkinameva ca|
anantaṃ viśvarūpaṃ ca tadbahiḥ pūjayet kramāt||
63||
Exact combined meaningOne should worship Vāsudeva and the rest in the quarters; then, beyond, Keśava and the rest; and outside of that, in order: Matsya, Kūrma, Varāha, Nārasiṁha and Vāmana, Bhārgava, Rāghava, Kṛṣṇa, Buddha and Kalkin, and Ananta and Viśvarūpa.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
"Vāsudeva and the rest" — Vāsudeva, Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna and Aniruddha. (61–62)
Chapter 1 · verses 64–65
1.64
अनन्तब्रह्मवाय्वीशान् वीशं चाग्रे प्रपूजयेत्।
वारुणीं चैव गायत्रीं भारतीं गिरिजामपि॥ ६४॥
anantabrahmavāyvīśān vīśaṃ cāgre prapūjayet|
vāruṇīṃ caiva gāyatrīṃ bhāratīṃ girijāmapi||
64||
1.65
कोणेषु वीन्द्रवामे च सौपर्णीं पूजयेदपि।
इन्द्रादीञ्छेषविध्यन्तान् सभार्यान् सपरिग्रहान्॥ ६५॥
koṇeṣu vīndravāme ca sauparṇīṃ pūjayedapi|
indrādīñcheṣavidhyantān sabhāryān saparigrahān||
65||
Exact combined meaningIn front one should duly worship Ananta, Brahmā, Vāyu, Īśa and the Lord of Birds; also Vāruṇī, Gāyatrī, Bhāratī and Girijā in the corners; and to the left of the Bird-lord one should worship Sauparṇī as well; (then) Indra and the rest, ending with Śeṣa and Vidhi, together with their wives and their retinues.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
"Vīśa" means Garuḍa. (63–64)
Chapter 1 · verse 66
1.66
धूपदीपौ ततो दत्वा नैवेद्यं मूलमन्त्रतः।
अनेन क्रमयोगेन जुहुयात् संस्कृतेऽनले॥ ६६॥
dhūpadīpau tato datvā naivedyaṃ mūlamantrataḥ|
anena kramayogena juhuyāt saṃskṛte’nale||
66||
Exact English meaningThen, having offered incense and lamp, and the naivedya with the mūla-mantra, one should offer oblations in the consecrated fire according to this same order.
Word and compound explanation
"Mūlamantrataḥ" — with the mūla-mantra. He states the fire-offering that follows the worship (anuyāga): "anena." Some say "anena" means by the order of the pīṭha-worship stated above; the knowers of the tradition say it means by the method about to be described. "In the consecrated (fire)" — consecrated with the rites prescribed in one's own śākhā. (65)
Chapter 1 · verse 67
1.67
पुष्पाञ्जलिश्च होमश्च मूलेनाष्टोत्तरं शतम्।
सकृत्सकृत् पुष्पमन्यैर्होमस्तस्य चतुर्गुणः॥ ६७॥
puṣpāñjaliśca homaśca mūlenāṣṭottaraṃ śatam|
sakṛtsakṛt puṣpamanyairhomastasya caturguṇaḥ||
67||
Exact English meaningHandfuls of flowers (puṣpāñjali) and homa: one hundred and eight with the mūla; with the other (mantras) a flower once each, the homa being fourfold thereof.
Word and compound explanation
With the mūla, the eight-syllable mantra: one hundred and eight puṣpāñjalis, and (as many) oblations. With the others — the twelve-syllable and the rest — a flower once each; the homa, however, is four times that of the flower(-offering). But others say: with the other mantras — those of the pīṭha and the āvaraṇas — the flower-offering is made once, while the homa is fourfold of that flower-offering. (66)
Chapter 1 · verse 68
1.68
विसर्जयित्वा नैवेद्यं मूलेन त्रिः समर्च्य च।
धूपदीपौ पुनर्दत्वा पुनर्मूलेन पूर्ववत्॥ ६८॥
visarjayitvā naivedyaṃ mūlena triḥ samarcya ca|
dhūpadīpau punardatvā punarmūlena pūrvavat||
68||
Exact English meaningHaving removed the naivedya and worshipped three times with the mūla, having again offered incense and lamp, (one should offer) once more with the mūla, as before.
Chapter 1 · verse 69
1.69
अर्चयित्वा हरिं ध्यात्वा जपेदष्टोत्तरं शतम्।
पुनर्ध्यायेद्धरिं सर्वदेवदेवेश्वरं प्रभुम्॥ ६९॥
arcayitvā hariṃ dhyātvā japedaṣṭottaraṃ śatam|
punardhyāyeddhariṃ sarvadevadeveśvaraṃ prabhum||
69||
Exact English meaningHaving worshipped and meditated once more, one should repeat (the mantra) one hundred and eight times; then meditate again on Hari, the Lord, the master of all the gods of gods.
Word and compound explanation
Having removed the naivedya, and having worshipped thrice — three times — with the mūla, the mūla-mantra, using sacred tulasī, flowers and the rest. (68)
Chapter 1 · verses 70–74
1.70
जपध्यानहुतार्चादीनेवं यः कुरुते सदा।
धर्मार्थकाममोक्षाणां भाजनं स्यात् स एव हि॥ ७०॥
japadhyānahutārcādīnevaṃ yaḥ kurute sadā|
dharmārthakāmamokṣāṇāṃ bhājanaṃ syāt sa eva hi||
70||
1.71
सर्वोत्तमं हरिं ज्ञात्वा य एवं भक्तिपूर्वकम्।
जपध्यानादिभिर्नित्यं पूजयेन्नास्य दुर्लभम्॥ ७१॥
sarvottamaṃ hariṃ jñātvā ya evaṃ bhaktipūrvakam|
japadhyānādibhirnityaṃ pūjayennāsya durlabham||
71||
1.72
भक्तिं कृत्वाऽन्यदेवेषु ब्रह्मरुद्रादिकेष्वपि।
सर्वोत्कर्षमविज्ञाय विष्णोर्याति तमो ध्रुवम्॥ ७२॥
bhaktiṃ kṛtvā’nyadeveṣu brahmarudrādikeṣvapi|
sarvotkarṣamavijñāya viṣṇoryāti tamo dhruvam||
72||
1.73
न यज्ञा नच तीर्थानि नोपवासव्रतानि च।
दैवतानि च सर्वाणि त्रातुं तं शक्नुयुः क्वचित्॥ ७३॥
na yajñā naca tīrthāni nopavāsavratāni ca|
daivatāni ca sarvāṇi trātuṃ taṃ śaknuyuḥ kvacit||
73||
1.74
हरिर्हि सर्वदेवानां परमः पूर्णशक्तिमान्।
स्वतन्त्रोऽन्ये तद्वशा हि सर्वेऽतः स जगद्गुरुः॥ ७४॥
harirhi sarvadevānāṃ paramaḥ pūrṇaśaktimān|
svatantro’nye tadvaśā hi sarve’taḥ sa jagadguruḥ||
74||
Exact combined meaningHe who ever performs japa, meditation, oblation, worship and the rest in this manner — he indeed becomes the vessel of dharma, artha, kāma and mokṣa. He who, knowing Hari as the highest of all, thus worships Him daily with devotion, with japa, meditation and the rest — for him nothing is hard to attain. But he who places devotion in other gods — even in Brahmā, Rudra and the rest — without recognizing Viṣṇu's supremacy over all, goes surely to darkness. Neither sacrifices, nor holy fords, nor fasts and vows, nor all the deities together, could ever save him. For Hari is supreme above all gods, possessed of complete power, independent; all others indeed are subject to Him — hence He is the guru of the universe.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
"Bhājana" means vessel. (69–73)

विष्णुसर्वोत्तमत्वज्ञानस्यावश्यकता

Chapter 1 · verse 75
1.75
ब्रह्मादयश्च तद्भक्त्या भागिनो भोगमोक्षयोः।
तस्माज्ज्ञेयश्च पूज्यश्च वन्द्यो ध्येयः सदा हरिः॥ ७५॥
brahmādayaśca tadbhaktyā bhāgino bhogamokṣayoḥ|
tasmājjñeyaśca pūjyaśca vandyo dhyeyaḥ sadā hariḥ||
75||
Exact English meaningBrahmā and the rest, through devotion to Him, become sharers in enjoyment and in liberation. Therefore Hari is ever to be known, to be worshipped, to be saluted, and to be meditated upon.
Word and compound explanation
Even Brahmā and the rest are sharers in enjoyment and liberation only through devotion to Him. Therefore Hari alone is to be known — to be worshipped, saluted, bowed to, praised, and meditated upon. (74)

Chapter 2द्वितीयोऽध्यायः

विष्णुसर्वोत्तमत्वज्ञानस्यावश्यकता

Chapter 2 · verses 1–2
2.1
यस्यकस्यापि मन्त्रस्य न्यासः पञ्चाशदक्षरैः।
वीर्यदः केऽभितो वक्त्रमक्षिकर्णेषु नासयोः॥ १॥
yasyakasyāpi mantrasya nyāsaḥ pañcāśadakṣaraiḥ|
vīryadaḥ ke’bhito vaktramakṣikarṇeṣu nāsayoḥ||
1||
2.2
गण्डयोरोष्ठयोर्दन्तपङ्क्त्योर्मूर्ध्नि च वाचि च।
दोःपत्सन्धिषु साग्रेषु पार्श्वयोः पृष्ठगुह्ययोः॥ २॥
gaṇḍayoroṣṭhayordantapaṅktyormūrdhni ca vāci ca|
doḥpatsandhiṣu sāgreṣu pārśvayoḥ pṛṣṭhaguhyayoḥ||
2||
Exact combined meaningFor any mantra whatsoever, the nyāsa with the fifty letters bestows power: on the head, around the face, on the eyes and ears, on the nostrils, on the cheeks, the lips, the two rows of teeth, on the crown and the organ of speech, on the joints of the arms and legs together with their tips, on the flanks, the back and the genitals,

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
In this chapter he expounds the mātṛkā-nyāsa, the prāṇāyāma, the worship of the kalaśa, and the rest, beginning with "yasya." The nyāsa with the fifty letters is (effective) "for any mantra whatsoever" — for every mantra, that is. "Vīryada": it bestows vīrya — power, whose mark is the deity's grace — hence "power-giving." "Ke" — on the head. "Abhito vaktram" — all around the face. (1–2)
Chapter 2 · verse 3
2.3
तुन्दे च हृदि धातूनां सप्तके प्राणजीवयोः।
सक्षकारान् न्यसेद् वर्णान् नृसिंहः क्षस्य देवता॥ ३॥
tunde ca hṛdi dhātūnāṃ saptake prāṇajīvayoḥ|
sakṣakārān nyased varṇān nṛsiṃhaḥ kṣasya devatā||
3||
Exact English meaningon the belly, and on the heart — (the seat) of the sevenfold bodily elements (dhātus) and of prāṇa and jīva — one should place the letters together with kṣa-kāra; Nṛsiṁha is the deity of kṣa.
Word and compound explanation
Of kṣa the deity is Narasiṁha. Together with it, the fifty-one letters, combined with their gods Aja and the rest, are to be placed. (3)
Chapter 2 · verse 4
2.4
जपे न्यासे च विहितः क्षोऽक्षराणां सदाऽन्ततः।
प्राणायामो रेचयित्वा पूरयित्वा च कुम्भके॥ ४॥
jape nyāse ca vihitaḥ kṣo’kṣarāṇāṃ sadā’ntataḥ|
prāṇāyāmo recayitvā pūrayitvā ca kumbhake||
4||
Exact English meaningIn japa and in nyāsa, kṣa is always enjoined at the end of the letters. The prāṇāyāma: having exhaled (recaka), having inhaled (pūraka), and in retention (kumbhaka).
Word and compound explanation
He states the prāṇāyāma: "prāṇāyāma." Recana is the downward expelling of the breath; pūraṇa, its drawing upward; kumbhaka, its restraint. (4)
Chapter 2 · verse 5
2.5
तारैस्त्रिर्द्वादशावृत्तैर्द्व्येकद्वादशकेन वा।
तत्तन्मन्त्रेण वा कार्यो गायत्र्या दशतारकैः॥ ५॥
tāraistrirdvādaśāvṛttairdvyekadvādaśakena vā|
tattanmantreṇa vā kāryo gāyatryā daśatārakaiḥ||
5||
Exact English meaningIt should be performed with the tāra repeated thrice twelve times, or with twice or once twelve; or with the mantra concerned; for the Gāyatrī, with ten tāras.
Word and compound explanation
With that praṇava repeated thrice twelve — twenty-six of them (sic: so printed; the arithmetic gives thirty-six); with twice twelve, twenty-four; or with a single twelve. The meaning is: prāṇāyāma with praṇavas of these numbers. "With the mantra concerned" — repeated twice, thrice, and so on. But for the Gāyatrī, "with ten tārakas" — supply: the prāṇāyāma is to be performed with ten praṇavas. Hence Yājñavalkya says: "Let him, with breath drawn out, recite three times the Gāyatrī together with the vyāhṛtis and the praṇava, along with the (Gāyatrī-)śiras — that is called prāṇāyāma." At the head of the seven vyāhṛtis, seven praṇavas; at the head of the Gāyatrī, one; (the śiras is) "Oṁ āpo jyotiḥ," etc. ( ) one at the beginning of the śiras-mantra, and one at the end. Thus, with the Gāyatrī joined to ten praṇavas, over the triad of recaka and the rest there are thirty praṇavas. (5)
Chapter 2 · verse 6
2.6
आदावेव जपे कुर्याच्छोषणं दहनं प्लुतिम्।
वाय्वग्निवारुणैर्बीजैर्ध्यात्वा तन्मण्डले हरिं॥ ६॥
ādāveva jape kuryācchoṣaṇaṃ dahanaṃ plutim|
vāyvagnivāruṇairbījairdhyātvā tanmaṇḍale hariṃ||
6||
Exact English meaningAt the very outset of japa one should perform the drying, the burning and the flooding with the seed-syllables of Vāyu, Agni and Varuṇa, meditating upon Hari in the maṇḍala of each.
Word and compound explanation
He states the purification of the elements (bhūtaśuddhi) that belongs to prāṇāyāma: "ādāv eva." When japa is to be performed, at the beginning one should carry out the drying and the rest with the bījas of Vāyu and the others. "In their maṇḍala" — in the maṇḍalas of Vāyu and the rest: having, with the bījas of Vāyu and Agni, performed the drying-up and the burning of the man of sin dwelling in the body, one should, with the bīja of Varuṇa, contemplate one's own body being flooded with nectar. (6)
Chapter 2 · verses 7–9
2.7
एकपञ्चाशद्वर्णानां चतुर्विंशतिमूर्तयः।
आत्माद्या वासुदेवाद्या विश्वाद्या मत्स्यकच्छपौ॥ ७॥
ekapañcāśadvarṇānāṃ caturviṃśatimūrtayaḥ|
ātmādyā vāsudevādyā viśvādyā matsyakacchapau||
7||
2.8
कोलो नृसिंहः सवटुर्जामदग्न्यरघूद्वहौ।
वासिष्ठयादवौ कृष्णावात्रेयो बुद्धकल्किनौ॥ ८॥
kolo nṛsiṃhaḥ savaṭurjāmadagnyaraghūdvahau|
vāsiṣṭhayādavau kṛṣṇāvātreyo buddhakalkinau||
8||
2.9
शिंशुमारश्चेति शतं कलाः कलशनामकाः।
एताभिः सहितां मूलमूर्तिं कुम्भोदके सुधीः॥ ९॥
śiṃśumāraśceti śataṃ kalāḥ kalaśanāmakāḥ|
etābhiḥ sahitāṃ mūlamūrtiṃ kumbhodake sudhīḥ||
9||
Exact combined meaning(The mūrtis) of the fifty-one letters; the twenty-four mūrtis; those beginning with Ātmā; those beginning with Vāsudeva; those beginning with Viśva; Matsya and the Tortoise, the Boar, Nṛsiṁha together with the Vaṭu (Vāmana), Jāmadagnya and the Bearer of the Raghu line, the two Kṛṣṇas — the Vāsiṣṭha (Vyāsa) and the Yādava — the son of Atri, Buddha and Kalkin, and Śiṁśumāra: these are the hundred kalās, named the kalaśa(-deities). Together with these, the wise one (should worship) the root-form in the water of the pot.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Word and compound explanation
He states the worship of the kalaśa: "ekapañcāśat." The mūrtis of the fifty-one letters, Aja and the rest; the twenty-four, Keśava and the rest; the four beginning with Ātmā; the four beginning with Vāsudeva; the four beginning with Viśva; and those beginning with Matsya and ending with Śiṁśumāra — a hundred kalās, that is, partial manifestations (aṁśas). (7–9)
Chapter 2 · verse 10
2.10
पूर्वोक्तविधिनाऽभ्यर्च्य प्रतिमां शिष्यमेव वा।
स्नापयेत् पूर्वमर्चायां जपोऽनूनः सहस्रतः॥ १०॥
pūrvoktavidhinā’bhyarcya pratimāṃ śiṣyameva vā|
snāpayet pūrvamarcāyāṃ japo’nūnaḥ sahasrataḥ||
10||
Exact English meaningHaving worshipped by the rule stated before, one should bathe the image — or the disciple; but beforehand, in the arcā, japa of not less than a thousand.
Word and compound explanation
Having worshipped, by the rule stated above — with the worship of the pīṭha, the invocation, the arghya and the other services — the root-form Nārāyaṇa in the arcā, the image, japa of not less than a thousand is to be performed before the bath. (10)
Chapter 2 · verse 11
2.11
मूलमन्त्रस्य चाङ्गानां न्यासः स्नानादनन्तरम्।
प्रतिमायां सन्निधिकृच्छिष्ये माहात्म्यकृद् भवेत्॥ ११॥
mūlamantrasya cāṅgānāṃ nyāsaḥ snānādanantaram|
pratimāyāṃ sannidhikṛcchiṣye māhātmyakṛd bhavet||
11||
Exact English meaningAfter the bath comes the nyāsa of the mūla-mantra and of its limbs: performed on the image, it effects (the Lord's) presence; on the disciple, it effects greatness.
Word and compound explanation
The nyāsa of the mūla-mantra — the eight-syllabled — and of its limbs, Kruddholka and the rest, after the bathing and the sprinkling: on the image it effects the presence of Hari; on the disciple, at the time of initiation, it effects greatness — it produces devotion, knowledge and the rest. (11)
Chapter 2 · verse 12
2.12
कलशः कीर्तिमायुष्यं प्रज्ञां मेधां श्रियं बलम्।
योग्यतां पापहानिं च पुण्यवृद्धिं च साधयेत्॥ १२॥
kalaśaḥ kīrtimāyuṣyaṃ prajñāṃ medhāṃ śriyaṃ balam|
yogyatāṃ pāpahāniṃ ca puṇyavṛddhiṃ ca sādhayet||
12||
Exact English meaningThe kalaśa(-rite) accomplishes fame, long life, wisdom, retentive intellect, fortune, lordship, fitness, the destruction of sin and the increase of merit.
Word and compound explanation
He states the subsidiary fruit of the kalaśa worship: "kalaśaḥ kīrtim." Prajñā means intelligence; medhā, the power of retention; bhaga, lordship; yogyatā, fitness — entitlement to the Lord's service and the like. (12)
Chapter 2 · verse 13
2.13
उपसर्गेषु जातेषु दैवभूतात्महेतुषु।
आयुषे वाऽथ शान्त्यै वा श्रिये वा पुण्यवृद्धये॥ १३॥
upasargeṣu jāteṣu daivabhūtātmahetuṣu|
āyuṣe vā’tha śāntyai vā śriye vā puṇyavṛddhaye||
13||
Exact English meaningWhen calamities have arisen — caused by the gods, by beings, or by oneself — or for long life, or for peace, or for fortune, or for the increase of merit,
Word and compound explanation
Now, in order to describe the special homa, he states its occasions: "upasargeṣu." Upasargas are afflictions. (13)
Chapter 2 · verse 14
2.14
योग्यतायै मन्त्रसिद्ध्यै विष्णोः प्रीत्यर्थमेव वा।
जुहुयात् सहस्रमयुतं लक्षं कोटिमथापिवा॥ १४॥
yogyatāyai mantrasiddhyai viṣṇoḥ prītyarthameva vā|
juhuyāt sahasramayutaṃ lakṣaṃ koṭimathāpivā||
14||
Exact English meaningor for fitness, or for the perfection of the mantra, or solely for the pleasure of Viṣṇu — one should offer a thousand, ten thousand, a hundred thousand, or even a crore of oblations.
Word and compound explanation
The option "a thousand" and so forth depends upon the smallness or magnitude of the occasion — or upon one's capacity. (14)
Chapter 2 · verse 15
2.15
पूर्ववत् संस्कृते वह्नौ ध्यात्वा देवं जनार्दनम्।
कृत्वा त्रिमेखलं कुण्डं चतुर्विंशाङ्गुलोच्छ्रितम्॥ १५॥
pūrvavat saṃskṛte vahnau dhyātvā devaṃ janārdanam|
kṛtvā trimekhalaṃ kuṇḍaṃ caturviṃśāṅgulocchritam||
15||
Exact English meaningIn a fire consecrated as before, having meditated upon the god Janārdana, having made a kuṇḍa with three girdles (mekhalās), raised twenty-four aṅgulas,
Word and compound explanation
"In the consecrated fire" — the connection with the foregoing is: one should offer oblations, first worshipping the deities of the pīṭha as before. "Trimekhala" — furnished with three ledges (vedis). "Raised twenty-four aṅgulas." (15)
Chapter 2 · verse 16
2.16
तावत् खातं चतुष्कोणमुच्छ्रितं द्वादशैव वा ।
द्व्यङ्गुलं त्र्यङ्गुलं वाऽपि चतुरङ्गुलमेव वा॥ १६॥
tāvat khātaṃ catuṣkoṇamucchritaṃ dvādaśaiva vā |
dvyaṅgulaṃ tryaṅgulaṃ vā’pi caturaṅgulameva vā||
16||
Exact English meaningdug to the same depth, four-cornered — or raised only twelve; (the girdles) two aṅgulas, three aṅgulas, or exactly four aṅgulas (broad),
Word and compound explanation
"Dug to that extent" — dug to the measure of twenty-four aṅgulas; "by twelve" — raised by a twelve of aṅgulas. (16)
Chapter 2 · verse 17
2.17
विस्तारो मेखलानां स्यादन्त्या वा चतुरङ्गुला।
चतुर्विंशाङ्गुलं वा तद्विस्तारो द्वादशैव वा॥ १७॥
vistāro mekhalānāṃ syādantyā vā caturaṅgulā|
caturviṃśāṅgulaṃ vā tadvistāro dvādaśaiva vā||
17||
Exact English meaningsuch should be the breadth of the mekhalās — or the last one four aṅgulas; or their breadth may be twenty-four aṅgulas, or just twelve.
Word and compound explanation
The breadth of the mekhalās is to be made two, three, or four aṅgulas; or else the last — that is, the third — is to be made four aṅgulas. Or the breadth of all three mekhalās together may be twenty-four aṅgulas, or twelve. (17)
Chapter 2 · verse 18
2.18
अश्वत्थपत्राकृतिः स्यान्मूलतो द्वादशाङ्गुला।
योनिः खाते च विनता प्रविष्टा द्व्यङ्गुलं तथा॥ १८॥
aśvatthapatrākṛtiḥ syānmūlato dvādaśāṅgulā|
yoniḥ khāte ca vinatā praviṣṭā dvyaṅgulaṃ tathā||
18||
Exact English meaningThe yoni should be of the shape of an aśvattha leaf, twelve aṅgulas from the base, bent downward, and entering likewise two aṅgulas into the pit.
Word and compound explanation
He states the characteristics of the yoni: shaped like an aśvattha leaf, from the base — beginning at the root — twelve aṅgulas, bent downward; the meaning is that its emplacement is to be made in the pit at two aṅgulas. The measure of the aṅgula, moreover, will be stated later in the words "barley-bellies laid crosswise," and so on. (18)
Chapter 2 · verse 19
2.19
तदात्मनि हरिं ध्यात्वा कुण्डे देवीं श्रियं तथा।
विष्णुवीर्यात्मकं वह्निं निक्षिपेत् प्रणवेन तु॥ १९॥
tadātmani hariṃ dhyātvā kuṇḍe devīṃ śriyaṃ tathā|
viṣṇuvīryātmakaṃ vahniṃ nikṣipet praṇavena tu||
19||
Exact English meaningHaving meditated on Hari as its very self, and likewise on the goddess Śrī in the kuṇḍa, one should cast in, with the praṇava, the fire that is of the essence of Viṣṇu's vigor.
Word and compound explanation
He states the installation of the fire: "tadātmani." "In its self" — having meditated upon Hari, and upon the goddess Śrī, in the kuṇḍa of the aforesaid character which is His form, one should cast in, with the praṇava, the fire which is of the essence of Viṣṇu's vigor. (19)
Chapter 2 · verse 20
2.20
कुर्यात् क्रियाः षोडश च व्याहृतीभिः पृथक्पृथक्।
वह्नेस्तद्गहरेः प्रीतिं कुर्वन् द्रव्यैर्यजेत् ततः॥ २०॥
kuryāt kriyāḥ ṣoḍaśa ca vyāhṛtībhiḥ pṛthakpṛthak|
vahnestadgahareḥ prītiṃ kurvan dravyairyajet tataḥ||
20||
Exact English meaningAnd one should perform the sixteen rites for the fire, each separately, with the vyāhṛtis; then, effecting thereby the pleasure of Hari whose form it is, one should sacrifice with the offering-substances.
Word and compound explanation
"Kuryāt": with the vyāhṛtis, severally and jointly, one should perform for the fire the sixteen saṁskāras, garbhādhāna and the rest. (As enumerated) in the Viṣṇutīrthīya: "Garbhādhāna, puṁsavana, sīmanta, the Vaiṣṇava bali; jātaka, the naming, the first going-out, the first feeding of rice, and the tonsure; the initiation (upanayana), the Mahānāmnī, and thereafter the Mahāvrata; the upākarma, the godāna, and thereafter the samāvartana; and marriage." Elsewhere too it is said that (the saṁskāras) ending with marriage are prescribed for the auspicious (rite), while in the inauspicious they end with the death-rites; on that view, be it noted, there is no Viṣṇu-bali. For each single saṁskāra one should offer four oblations apiece. (20)
Chapter 2 · verse 21
2.21
आज्येन वा पायसेन समिद्भिः क्षीरिणामथ।
तिलैर्वा तण्डुलैर्वाऽपि मधुरैस्त्रिभिरेव वा॥ २१॥
ājyena vā pāyasena samidbhiḥ kṣīriṇāmatha|
tilairvā taṇḍulairvā’pi madhuraistribhireva vā||
21||
Exact English meaningWith clarified butter, or with pāyasa, or with fuel-sticks of the milky trees, or with sesame, or even with rice-grains, or with just the three sweets.
Word and compound explanation
He states the substances for the homa: "ājyena." With ājya from the cow; or with pāyasa cooked in cow's milk; with fuel-sticks of the milky trees, the udumbara and the rest; "with the three sweets" — ghee, honey, sugar, and the like. (21)
Chapter 2 · verse 22
2.22
ब्रह्मवृक्षसमिद्भिर्वा समिद्भिर्वाऽमृतोद्भवैः।
अमृताः समिधो ज्ञेयाः सर्वत्र चतुरङ्गुलाः॥ २२॥
brahmavṛkṣasamidbhirvā samidbhirvā’mṛtodbhavaiḥ|
amṛtāḥ samidho jñeyāḥ sarvatra caturaṅgulāḥ||
22||
Exact English meaningOr with fuel-sticks of the brahma-tree, or with sticks sprung from the amṛtā; the amṛtā-sticks are to be known, in every case, as four aṅgulas long.
Word and compound explanation
The brahma-tree is the palāśa. "Amṛtāḥ" — fuel-sticks of the guḍūcī; measuring four aṅgulas in all homas. (22)
Chapter 2 · verses 23–24
2.23
एवमेव च दीक्षायामाज्येनैवाहुतिक्रिया।
पूजा च कार्या विधिवत् पूर्वं सर्वहुतेष्वपि॥ २३॥
evameva ca dīkṣāyāmājyenaivāhutikriyā|
pūjā ca kāryā vidhivat pūrvaṃ sarvahuteṣvapi||
23||
2.24
गुरवे दक्षिणां दद्याल्लक्षेलक्षे शतं सुधीः।
आत्मनैव कृते होमे शक्तितो गुरुदक्षिणा॥ २४॥
gurave dakṣiṇāṃ dadyāllakṣelakṣe śataṃ sudhīḥ|
ātmanaiva kṛte home śaktito gurudakṣiṇā||
24||
Exact combined meaningIn just the same way, at initiation (dīkṣā) the offering of oblations is with ājya alone; and in all fire-offerings too, worship must first be duly performed according to rule. The wise man should give the teacher a fee of one hundred for every hundred thousand (oblations); when the homa has been performed by oneself, (one should still give) the guru's fee according to one's means.

These verses form one translation unit because the commentary edition joins their Sanskrit syntax across different verse boundaries.

Chapter 3तृतीयोऽध्यायः

प्रतिमाद्रव्यादि

Chapter 3 · verse 1
3.1
प्रतिष्ठां कारयन् विष्णोः कुर्यात् सुप्रतिमां बुधः।
लोहैर्वा शिलया दार्वा मृदा वाऽपि यथाक्रमम्॥ १॥
pratiṣṭhāṃ kārayan viṣṇoḥ kuryāt supratimāṃ budhaḥ|
lohairvā śilayā dārvā mṛdā vā’pi yathākramam||
1||
Exact English meaningThe wise man, when having an installation (pratiṣṭhā) of Viṣṇu performed, should have a fine image made — of metals, or of stone, or of wood, or even of clay, in that order of preference.
Word and compound explanation
Wishing to expound the installation of images, he states in this chapter the characteristics of the image: "pratiṣṭhām." "Causing the installation" — being about to have it performed. "With metals" — with gold and the other metals. "Of wood" — with sandal or other such timber. (1)
Chapter 3 · verse 2
3.2
षण्णवत्यङ्गुलां योग आसीनामथवा स्थिताम्।
शयानां वा मुमुक्षूणां व्याचक्षाणां निजां स्थितिम्॥ २॥
ṣaṇṇavatyaṅgulāṃ yoga āsīnāmathavā sthitām|
śayānāṃ vā mumukṣūṇāṃ vyācakṣāṇāṃ nijāṃ sthitim||
2||
Exact English meaningNinety-six aṅgulas tall; seated in yoga, or else standing, or reclining — proclaiming to the seekers of liberation their own (true) state.
Word and compound explanation
"Measuring ninety-six aṅgulas." "In yoga" — seated in the yoga posture. "Proclaiming to the seekers of liberation their own state" — declaring (to each) his own essential nature; or the construction is: "reclining, and proclaiming." (2)

अवयवपरिमाणादि

Chapter 3 · verse 3
3.3
मुखादूर्ध्वं द्व्यङ्गुलोच्चां नवाङ्गुललसन्मुखाम्।
सुवृत्तत्र्यङ्गुलग्रीवामा स्तनाच्च षडङ्गुलाम्॥ ३॥
mukhādūrdhvaṃ dvyaṅguloccāṃ navāṅgulalasanmukhām|
suvṛttatryaṅgulagrīvāmā stanācca ṣaḍaṅgulām||
3||
Exact English meaningAbove the face, two aṅgulas high (the crown); her shining face of nine aṅgulas; her neck well-rounded, of three aṅgulas; and six aṅgulas down to the breast;
Word and compound explanation
He sets forth the make-up of the ninety-six aṅgulas, from "mukhād ūrdhvam" down to "sahārdha-caturaṅgulām." Above the face — up to the top of the head — two aṅgulas high; that (image) whose gleaming face measures nine aṅgulas; whose neck is well-rounded, measuring three aṅgulas; "and down to the breast" — six aṅgulas from below the neck to the breast. (3)
Chapter 3 · verse 4
3.4
पञ्चादशाङ्गुलां नाभेरा सार्धदशकाङ्गुलाम्।
वृषणादा मूलतश्च सार्धद्व्यङ्गुलमायताम्॥ ४॥
pañcādaśāṅgulāṃ nābherā sārdhadaśakāṅgulām|
vṛṣaṇādā mūlataśca sārdhadvyaṅgulamāyatām||
4||
Exact English meaningfifteen aṅgulas to the navel; from there to the scrotum ten and a half aṅgulas; and from (the scrotum) to the root, two and a half aṅgulas in extent;
Word and compound explanation
"From the navel" — that is, up to the navel, below the breast: fifteen aṅgulas. "From the scrotum" — up to the scrotum, below the navel: ten and a half aṅgulas. "Āmūlataḥ" — as far as the mūlādhāra: two and a half aṅgulas in extent. The word-division is "ā mūlataḥ" (with the prefix ā); or else the one prefix āṅ connects with both terms, by the maxim of the lamp set on the threshold (serving both rooms). (4)
Chapter 3 · verse 5
3.5
तावदा गुदतो दीर्घां चैत्यादष्टादशाङ्गुलाम्।
तथा द्व्यङ्गुलचैत्यां च द्वाविंशज्जङ्घया युताम्॥ ५॥
tāvadā gudato dīrghāṃ caityādaṣṭādaśāṅgulām|
tathā dvyaṅgulacaityāṃ ca dvāviṃśajjaṅghayā yutām||
5||
Exact English meaningas much again in length down to the anus; eighteen aṅgulas from the caitya (knee); likewise with a knee-region of two aṅgulas, and furnished with a shank of twenty-two;
Word and compound explanation
"From the anus" — up to the apāna: that much, two and a half aṅgulas long. "From the caitya" — from the knee: (the thigh) reaching to the knee, eighteen aṅgulas. "Likewise with a two-aṅgula caitya" — having a knee-region measuring two aṅgulas; joined with a shank measuring twenty-two aṅgulas. (5)
Chapter 3 · verses 6–9
3.6
आरभ्य गुल्फमध्यं च सहार्धचतुरङ्गुलाम्।
षडङ्गुलोच्चप्रपदां सार्धत्र्यङ्गुलमेव च।
प्रपदोच्चयुतामन्ते द्व्यङ्गुलप्रपदोच्छ्रयाम्॥ ६॥
ārabhya gulphamadhyaṃ ca sahārdhacaturaṅgulām|
ṣaḍaṅguloccaprapadāṃ sārdhatryaṅgulameva ca|
prapadoccayutāmante dvyaṅgulaprapadocchrayām||
6||
3.7
त्र्यङ्गुलद्व्यङ्गुलामर्धहीनमध्याङ्गुलामपि।
त्रिपदाङ्गुलहीनान्यां ततस्तादृशतत्पराम्॥ ७॥
tryaṅguladvyaṅgulāmardhahīnamadhyāṅgulāmapi|
tripadāṅgulahīnānyāṃ tatastādṛśatatparām||
7||
3.8
पादोनोच्चाङ्गुष्ठनखां तदर्धतदनन्तराम्।
क्रमशः पादहीनान्यां रक्तपादनखां शुभाम्॥ ८॥
pādonoccāṅguṣṭhanakhāṃ tadardhatadanantarām|
kramaśaḥ pādahīnānyāṃ raktapādanakhāṃ śubhām||
8||
3.9
विस्तारः प्रपदस्यापि षडङ्गुल उदाहृतः।
अङ्गुष्ठपरिणाहस्तु चतुरङ्गुल ईरितः।
त्र्यङ्गुलस्तु तदन्यस्य व्रीह्यर्धोनाः क्रमात् पराः॥ ९॥
vistāraḥ prapadasyāpi ṣaḍaṅgula udāhṛtaḥ|
aṅguṣṭhapariṇāhastu caturaṅgula īritaḥ|
tryaṅgulastu tadanyasya vrīhyardhonāḥ kramāt parāḥ||
9||
Exact combined meaningand, beginning from the middle of the ankle, four and a half aṅgulas; the fore-foot six aṅgulas high — and (its middle) just two and a half. joined with the fore-foot's height — at its end an elevation of two aṅgulas; her pair of toes (the great toe and the next) of three aṅgulas; and the middle toe less by a half. the next (toe) less by three quarters of an aṅgula; then, following it, one of like measure (less a quarter); the nail of her great toe three quarters (of an aṅgula) high, and the one next to it half of that. the others, in order, each less by a quarter; her toe-nails red; auspicious. And the breadth of the fore-foot is declared to be six aṅgulas.
